11. **A FIGURE WITH NO OWNER IS A FIGURE ANYBODY CAN ADD TO, AND ON THE SIXTY-SECOND DAY OF THE LONG MONTH A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON THE OPEN HAND BOARD COUNTED OFF THE FIVE-FIFTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, WHICH IS WHERE FOUR PEOPLE AND A QUESTION WERE NAMED IN CHAPTER 0145, AND SAID SEVENTY-TWO DAYS OUT LOUD IN A MARKET, AND IT IS THE FIRST FIGURE ANYBODY HAS PUT ON THE DELEGATION.** Said once, in a mouth, in daylight, in a market, and attached to a decision, which is that he will not say it again and will not add to it and will not correct it. **A woman of about twenty-four wrote it down and rubbed it out in about a second and gave the reason, which is that she does not own the figure, and a man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom said the sentence in the same market: a figure with no owner is a figure anybody can add to.** A delegation is a number with legs and a number with legs and a figure against it is a delay, and **the only instrument that could take the figure back belongs to a woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register and who has it in her own pocket with a date on the back of it and two names on the front of it and no third, and she will not carry it a fourth time, and nobody in this batch asked her about the four and nobody in this batch asked whether they will go.** *Open and nobody may ask and nobody may answer it.*

12. **A SACK CAME UP A BANK ELEVEN MILES FROM THIS CITY ON THE SIXTY-FIRST DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, DAY 216, A SUNDAY, AND A MAN OF ABOUT FORTY WHO CARRIES SACKS PUT IT DOWN IN A YARD AND DID NOT GO UP TO THE DOOR, AND IT IS THE FOURTH HE HAS CARRIED AND HE HAS COUNTED IT BECAUSE THAT IS HIS TRADE AND NOT BECAUSE IT IS A QUESTION.** Nobody in that house has ever asked him where it came from and he has never asked, and he will not be there for the next one. **A man of about fifty-two who digs came down that same bank that day for a rod he never had, and a man of about forty-four who keeps a stall came up it with a basket he did not need to bring, worked out what he would write at the end of a line for a sack, found there is no figure at the end of that sentence, and did not open a twelfth entry in his column of costs.** *The cost column's one paid field is the thirteenth day of the new month, day 168, the column did not move, and there is still no twelfth entry and nothing recited in these ten chapters.*

13. **NOBODY IN THAT HOUSE HAS EVER COUNTED THE SACKS AND NEITHER HAS THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-NINE WHO KEEPS IT, AND THAT IS ON PURPOSE, AND THE REASON SHE GIVES IN HER OWN WORDS IS THAT A COUNT OF SACKS IS A COUNT OF WHAT THE DAYS BOUGHT AND THE DAY SOMEBODY COUNTS THAT IS THE DAY IT IS A PRICE, AND IT IS NOT A PRICE, IT IS A SACK ON A STEP.** The days went back on the forty-first day of the Long Month, in daylight, on a flat, unasked. **Nobody in that yard asked her the other thing, and about nine people in it have said since that they were not going to be the one, and that a woman who carries sacks up a bank and does not come up to the door is the reason nobody had to.** *Open on purpose. No chapter may ask and nobody may answer it and nobody may count them.*

14. **A SLATE WITH A DATE ON IT WENT FACE DOWN ON A CHEST IN THE BACK OF A STORE ON THE SIXTY-FOURTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, DAY 219, AND NOBODY WROTE A REPLACEMENT DATE, AND IT WAS THE ONLY PAGE IN THIS CITY WITH A DATE ON IT THAT ANYBODY COULD READ.** A woman of about thirty-four in that store, who is on no list in it, said the reason in a yard in front of about four people, and it is that a slate with a day in the past on it is a thing about the past and there is no next date and she is not going to write one. **She asked the date in four words at the seventh hour of the evening on the thirtieth day of the Long Month, day 185, and was told nothing, and she said that out loud on the sixty-fourth, and a woman of about twenty-four had her hand on a slate and was asked whether she wanted it written down and was told no and wrote it down anyway, and neither of them said the word refusal.** A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall said in a yard that a date is not a clock, that there has not been a clock in this city since the thirty-fourth day of the Long Month, and that he was one of about four people who had been calling a slate a clock because they wanted one. **A page can be taken down. A slate can be turned over. She is still a person somebody can ask, and about four people who had come nine miles to read a date asked her what it was and she said it.** *Active. A later writer may not put the slate back up and may not write a new date on a slate.*

15. **ABOUT NINE PEOPLE CAN NOW FETCH A MAN BY A NAME HE DID NOT CHOOSE, AND ON THE SIXTY-FOURTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH HE WALKED NINE MILES INTO A YARD WHERE ABOUT NINE PEOPLE COULD HAVE SAID IT AND NOT ONE OF THEM DID, AND HE DID NOT SAY IT HIMSELF, AND HE STOOD THERE FOR ABOUT NINE MINUTES AND WAS NOT FETCHED.** He was asked nothing about a gate and nothing else, and he was offered nothing, and he came because he wanted to see the other half of it, and what the other half of it was is a woman turning a slate over. **Being unfetched is not the same as being unfindable and he is the only person in that yard who knows which of those two those are and he is not going to explain it to anybody.** *Active. He remains Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing.*

16. **THE FIGURES, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM IS DERIVED FROM AN ANCHOR AND NOT FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER.** The Long Month day number is the chapter's own day less 155. The trestle count is the day less forty: one hundred and seventy-two at Chapter 0212, one hundred and sixty-five written on the fiftieth day of the Long Month and still the newest figure on the wall behind him, one hundred and fifty-eight at the top of that column for the forty-third day of the Long Month. The posting went up on day 201 and the days it has been up are the day less 201: ten at Chapter 0211 to nineteen at Chapter 0220. The days the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid are the day less eighty-five: a hundred and twenty-six at Chapter 0211 and no other figure in these ten chapters. The causeway has had nothing on it since the morning of the forty-second day of the Long Month, day 197: seventeen at Chapter 0214. The water stopped coming down the channel on the forty-first day of the Long Month, day 196: eighteen at Chapter 0214 and twenty at Chapter 0216. The keeper's year began on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, day 179, and her days into it are the Long Month day less twenty-four: thirty-seven at Chapter 0216, in narration and in no mouth. The share with the post taken out was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and is fifty-one days in a coat at Chapter 0219. The delegation was named on the fifty-fifth day of the month after this one, day 145, and is seventy-two days old at Chapter 0217. The interval since the seventh week of this flood and the interval since the thirty-seventh day of the Long Month are written as anchors in this batch and not as figures. **The count of this city's silences is one hundred and fourteen at day 210 and one hundred and twenty-four at day 220, both derived, and it is on no page in these ten chapters, and the wall said nothing on all ten days and there was no panel.**

17. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE AND DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IN CHAPTERS 0211 TO 0220 WAS RECOMPUTED FROM DAY 1 AND NOT FROM CHAPTER 0210.** Ten chapters, ten days, days 211 to 220: 211 Tuesday, 212 Wednesday, 213 Thursday, 214 Friday, 215 Saturday, 216 Sunday, 217 Monday, 218 Tuesday, 219 Wednesday, 220 Thursday. **Every interior weekday word in the ten was extracted by script and read against the day it names, and all of them are correct. The three weekly lines in the trestle book have all fallen on a Wednesday, and the forty-third and fiftieth days of the Long Month are both Wednesdays, and the man who keeps that number did not know it and nobody had asked him and the handle was on the figure the whole time.** Day 220 carries one chapter and there is no double day in this batch, and **no chapter runs past day 220, so the form after the fourth is not established here and the question of what the form after the fourth one is is not asked by this batch and is not answered by it.**

18. **NOTHING IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS IS A POWER.** The gains are a chest opened in a yard, a Wednesday nobody had looked at, a ninth of a weight on a slate, a rod in a hole nobody has measured, a figure with no owner, a sack that nobody has counted, a slate turned face down, a word with a wall in it that cannot be corrected, and a man who said the second half of his own sentence and did nothing about it. **Adrian remains Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing. He performed no working, no threshold was opened, the Narrow Mark was not opened and was not looked at, in the ninth batch, the other world is not named once in the ten chapters, no chapter offered him a workway and he asked for none, no chapter turned the name he did not choose into a qualification, and no chapter asked him about a gate.**

---

## 29. Rules established in Volume 05, Chapters 0221–0230

**AND THIS SECTION WENT THROUGH THE REVIEW REPAIR PASS OF `reviews/volume-05/batch-0003.md`, and the disposition is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0003.md` §17. NOT ONE FIGURE, DATE, ANCHOR, DAY, BEAT, CAST MEMBER, ENDING OR THREAD STATE IN IT MOVED, and four statements in it were wrong about the page rather than wrong about the world and are annotated where they stand: item 2's date for the crack running out under the rim, item 8's two share figures, and item 9's three chains, of which only a handful of their values are in the prose at all.**

**These are additions to the sections above and not replacements. They are on the page in the third ten days of Volume 05. §23 is not changed, because §23.19 is the close of Volume 03; §24 is not changed, because §24 is the close of the first twenty chapters of Volume 04; §25 is not changed; §26 is not changed, because §26 is the close of Volume 04; §27 is not changed, because §27 is the close of Volume 05's first ten days; and §28 is not changed, because §28 is the close of Volume 05's second ten days. The volume's close record is still `state/volume-04-close.md`, the batch's record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0003.md`, and `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this section does not decide what Volume 05 is.**

**AND THIS BATCH RAN PAST DAY 220 AND THEREFORE FACED THE END OF THE FOURTH DATE FORM. The fourth form is *the Nth day of the Long Month* and the Nth is day 155 + N, and it is on the page in `bible/power-system.md` §24 for days 156 to 220. Chapters 0221 to 0230 are days 221 to 230, which is the sixty-sixth to the seventy-fifth day of the Long Month, and every event in these ten chapters that belongs to a day after 220 has been entered in the fourth form and in no other form, and NO FIFTH FORM HAS BEEN ESTABLISHED BY THIS SECTION, and no chapter of this batch carries this line, and a later phase that needs one has to decide it in a place allowed to decide it.**

1. **THE SALT IN THE SETTLEMENT'S CHEST WENT HARD.** It is the settlement's own salt off the settlement's own racks, it came out of that chest on the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month, day 211, and on the sixty-sixth day of that month, day 221, a man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board put a bar into a quarter of a sack he had paid a figure for on the sixty-second day of that month and the bar stopped, and the sack was one piece. **A sack you can pour is a sack; a piece does not have a part of it, and a figure a person has to break something to get is not a figure a person can put their hand on.** A ninth of a sack was the only figure that has ever existed under any of the three prices, it went onto a beam and two pans on the fifty-eighth day of that month, and a quarter of a sack went onto the same beam on the sixty-seventh and weighed what it weighed and could not be divided. *A chapter may not say what a wage in salt is worth and none of these ten does.*

2. **THE BEAM CAN TELL YOU WHAT A THING IS AND CANNOT TELL YOU WHAT A PART OF A THING IS, AND ON THE SEVENTY-THIRD DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, DAY 228, ITS FAR PAN CAME APART IN A YARD IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINE PEOPLE.** The crack had been in that pan since the fifty-ninth day of that month, it ran out under the rim before the sixty-seventh, and it was told to one yard on the sixty-seventh day of that month and to nobody since. **The review repair pass is what separated those, because `chapter-0228.md` had three ages for one object in one chapter — the fifty-ninth, the sixty-seventh, and six days — and a crack cannot be younger than the chapter that shows it already out under the rim. At the seventy-third it is fourteen days old and the secret is six.** **A beam with one pan on it is a thing that can do one of the two things it was made to do, and the man who made it left it in that yard on the seventy-third day of that month and did not carry it home, and he has carried it nine miles since the fifty-eighth day of that month and a beam that can only do one of those is not a beam he would carry nine miles.** The two pieces of the pan are wood and about four people in this city have worked out inside three days that every length of wood anybody in this city has been holding for about nine days came off a rack. *Open. A later writer may not replace the pan and may not have him carry the beam back.*

3. **A RACK IS FOUR LENGTHS OF WOOD AND A ROPE AND FOUR PEOPLE, ABOUT NINE OF THE RACKS CAN BE WORKED, EVERY ONE OF THOSE NINE IS ON SOMEBODY'S ROTA, AND THE ONLY OTHER LENGTHS OF WOOD IN THIS CITY ARE ON THOSE FRAMES.** Said out loud in a yard on the seventy-second day of the Long Month by a man who has carried racks up and down a cut since the second month of this flood, and he said *off a rack* and would not say which rack and would not say the beam. **A length of wood came up a bank eleven miles from this city on that day and was left lying on the boards of a yard, and a man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board stood at the edge of that yard and looked at it and did not ask for it and did not take it, and a man of about thirty-two stood at the top of the cut and said in his own mouth that he had thought about taking one off a rack and had not, and a woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder lifted one end of it about four inches off the boards and put it down again and said nothing about it.** *Open. Nobody took the wood.*

4. **A DIRECTION IS A FIGURE WITH THE DAY LEFT OFF IT, AND ON THE SIXTY-NINTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, DAY 224, A WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR FROM A FOURTH HOUSEHOLD ON THE ROAD SAID THE SEVENTY-FIFTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH OUT LOUD AT THE TOP OF A CUT TO THE MAN WHO HAD SAID FOUR WEEKS, AND HE DID NOT KNOW THERE WAS A NUMBER.** He said it twice, in a market on a causeway on the forty-seventh day of the Long Month, and both times it was *in about four weeks* and both times he said that four weeks is a direction and not a figure, and he said that about nine people in this city have written the number down instead. **Four weeks from the forty-seventh day of the Long Month is the seventy-fifth day of that month, which is day 230, and that day came on the seventieth day of this batch and nothing came with it, and there has been no water in that cut since the forty-first day of the Long Month and there was none on the seventy-fifth.** He put a rod down flat on the cracked bottom of that cut on the sixty-ninth day of that month and did not pick it up again, and on the seventy-fifth he said out loud at the bottom of the dry channel that he has never once said what the four weeks was a direction about. *Open and not resolved, and a chapter may not resolve it.*

5. **A WAGE IN SALT IS GIVEN TO A PERSON AND NOT TO A FIGURE OF PERSONS, AND NOBODY COUNTED THE SACKS THAT CAME OUT OF THE CHEST.** A man of about thirty-four in a market four streets from a wharf asked how many came out of it on the sixty-eighth day of the Long Month and was told nobody knew, and the reason given was that a person is given a sack and not a figure of sacks. **A woman of about thirty-four who took that wage carried it nine miles on her arm for ten days and had put her thumb in the seam of it four times and had not opened it, and a man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close carried a wage four streets twice and did not open it, and a woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market has had nine measures under her counter hard since the thirty-ninth day of that month and paid a figure for them on the twelfth day of that month and has not opened them either.** *Open. Nobody in four hundred miles knows how many sacks came out of that chest and the sacks in the house at the bottom of the Redroot cut are a different set of sacks and have still never been counted and nobody may count them.*

6. **THE QUEUE RAN ON A TUESDAY ON THE SEVENTIETH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, DAY 225, WITH NOTHING TO BE PAID OUT OF THE CHEST, AND THE BOARD WAS CORRECT AND SILENT AND THE NINTH LINE WAS THE ONLY REASON ANYBODY CAME.** It is the **fourth** Tuesday that queue has run on counting from the forty-ninth day of the Long Month, which is the one it formed on, and the fifty-sixth was the second and the sixty-third was the third, and the seventieth is the second one it has run on with salt in that chest instead of a wage. **The man of about twenty-seven who washes that board washed it and wrote nothing and left the figures of the day before standing, which was a Monday on which about nine people were paid, and his reason is that a blank board is a board that says nothing happened on a day, and about nine people did not happen on that Monday.** The woman of about thirty-four in that store did not open the chest and said in a yard that a chest that is opened in a yard is a wage and a chest that is not opened is not one, and that she is not a person who gets to say which. The man of about thirty-four who wrote the page said he is not going to be the man who says whether that line works on a morning when there is nothing at the front of it. *Whether the ninth line works is still unanswered and may not be resolved by a later batch. The posting is now twenty-nine days old and no figure is on the wall.*

7. **A MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE FROM THE SETTLEMENT'S WORKROOM WROTE DOWN ON THE SEVENTIETH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH THAT HE IS NOT GOING TO SAY ADRIAN VALE'S NAME, WITH THE DATE AT THE BOTTOM OF IT AS THE DAY HE WROTE IT AND NOT AS THE DAY IT STOPS.** He put the slate face up on the boards of a yard, which is where he has carried it since the forty-sixth day of that month, and about four people in that yard watched him do it and about nine of them had been carrying a slate for him for twenty-four days and had never been asked to put it down, and he picked it up about a minute later and put it back under his arm. **It is the first decision anybody in this city has written down against himself, and a decision on a page is a page, and he said out loud in the same yard that he could not tell that morning why this was different and that about nine people in this city have written down a decision in a book since the second day of this flood and not one of them has been on the other side of one.** Nobody fetched anybody that morning. *Active, and it does not make him unfetchable, and about nine people in this city can still fetch Adrian Vale by a name he did not choose.*

8. **ADRIAN VALE IS IN FOUR OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS AND DID ORDINARY WORK IN ALL FOUR, AND IS STILL STAGE 2, PASSAGE HOLDER, IN THE DOING.** He picked a lump of salt up off a pan with both arms and carried it about nine feet and put it down and his hands went white at the prints, and he walked nine miles to a yard on a Tuesday with nothing to be paid and said out loud that he had been wrong about a queue, and **he moved eleven sacks along a wall of a back room on the seventy-first day of the Long Month and did not lift the twelfth and has said in his own words that he came up nine miles and did not help**, and he went down into a dry channel on the seventy-fifth day of that month and said that the thing he saw was a channel and not a sentence. **A share with the post taken out is fifty-three days in a coat at Chapter 0221 and sixty-two days at Chapter 0230 — BOTH DERIVED FROM THE THIRTEENTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH AND NEITHER ON A PAGE; the only figure in a mouth is fifty-eight, twice, in Chapter 0226 — and is unanswered, and he took it out of the coat in a room on the seventy-first day of that month and put it back in about the time it takes to turn a thing over.** No chapter of this batch offered him a workway and he asked for none, no chapter turned the name he did not choose into a qualification, no chapter asked him about a gate, no threshold was opened, he performed no working, and the other world is not named once in the ten chapters.

9. **THE FIGURES, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM IS DERIVED FROM AN ANCHOR AND NOT FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER.** The Long Month day number is the chapter's own day less 155. The posting went up on day 201 and the days it has been up are the day less 201: twenty at Chapter 0221 to twenty-nine at Chapter 0230, **and the only two of those values that are on a page are the twenty-two and the twenty-four, both in section leads, and the rest is derived and a later writer may use it and may not attribute it to a chapter or to a mouth.** The days the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid are the day less eighty-five and **no figure is on a page in these ten chapters and the men are named in a mouth in Chapter 0225 without one.** The channel has had no water since the forty-first day of the Long Month, day 196, and the days it has been dry are the day less 196: twenty-five at Chapter 0221 to thirty-four at Chapter 0230, **and the only three of those values that are on a page are the twenty-eighth, the thirty-first and the thirty-four, and the other eight are derived.** The causeway has had nothing on it since the forty-second day of the Long Month, day 197: twenty-four at Chapter 0221 to thirty-three at Chapter 0230, and it is named in a mouth once in Chapter 0224, **and the review repair pass found that the day the causeway has stood empty is on no page in these ten chapters beyond that one mouth, which is the same species of claim as the three chains above and is now labelled rather than implied.** The keeper's year began on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, day 179, and her days into it are the Long Month day less twenty-four, and she is on a page once in these ten chapters, on the seventy-fourth day of that month, at fifty days, in narration and in no mouth. The share with the post taken out was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and the day less 168 is its figure at any day: fifty-three at 221, fifty-eight at 226 and sixty-two at 230, **of which only the fifty-eight is spoken.** The trestle count is the day less forty and **is on no page in these ten chapters and no chapter may put a figure on it.** The interval since the seventh week of this flood, the interval since the forty-eighth day of the Long Month and the interval since the fifty-sixth day of that month are written as anchors in this batch and not as figures, and **the count of this city's silences is one hundred and thirty-four at day 230, derived, on no page, and the wall said nothing on all ten days and there was no panel.**

10. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE AND DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IN CHAPTERS 0221 TO 0230 WAS RECOMPUTED FROM DAY 1 AND NOT FROM CHAPTER 0220.** Ten chapters, ten days, days 221 to 230: 221 Friday, 222 Saturday, 223 Sunday, 224 Monday, 225 Tuesday, 226 Wednesday, 227 Thursday, 228 Friday, 229 Saturday, 230 Sunday. **Every interior weekday word in the ten was extracted by script and read against the day it names and all of them are correct.** The forty-ninth, the fiftieth and the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month are Tuesdays and the sixty-third and the seventieth are Tuesdays, and the twenty-fourth day of that month is a Friday and is barred from carrying a weekday while the conflict at `bible/power-system.md` §27.14 stands, and no chapter of this batch attaches one to it. Day 230 carries one chapter and there is no double day in this batch.

11. **NOTHING IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS IS A POWER.** The gains are a lump instead of a quarter of a sack, a beam that can weigh a thing and not divide it, a pan in two pieces with nobody taking the wood, a date nobody chose arriving with nothing in it, a count of sacks that was never taken, a board that is correct and silent, a slate put face up on a board for a minute, a groove worn in a floor, and a sack that did not come up a bank. **The compact of eleven lines is not ended. The provisional recognition is not ended. No form for *not recognised* has been found. The seat of holder of the pull is vacant. The delegation of four people and a question has not left and nobody has been asked whether they will go, and no figure in this batch is on it. The room at the far end of the Narrow Mark is unlooked at, in the tenth batch, and this batch did not go and look. The box is shut. The boards under the chapel are not lifted. The twenty-fifth reading of the join-line is not taken. The trestle book still has a line in it that is not a number and the man keeping the number has still not mentioned it and no chapter of this batch had anybody ask him about it. The right of refusal that cannot be bought out is not filled and the hole in the Reach's own rule is not filled and the two are not joined, and a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms is not mentioned once in these ten chapters.**

---

## 30. Rules established in Volume 05, Chapters 0231–0240

**These are additions to the sections above and not replacements. They are on the page in the fourth ten days of Volume 05. §23 is not changed, because §23.19 is the close of Volume 03; §24 is not changed, because §24 is the close of the first twenty chapters of Volume 04; §25 is not changed; §26 is not changed, because §26 is the close of Volume 04; §27 is not changed, because §27 is the close of Volume 05's first ten days; §28 is not changed, because §28 is the close of Volume 05's second ten days; and §29 is not changed, because §29 is the close of Volume 05's third ten days. The volume's close record is still `state/volume-04-close.md`, the batch's record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0004.md`, and `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this section does not decide what Volume 05 is.**

**AND THIS BATCH RAN PAST DAY 220 FOR THE SECOND TIME AND FACED THE END OF THE FOURTH DATE FORM AGAIN. The fourth form is *the Nth day of the Long Month* and the Nth is day 155 + N, and it is on the page in `bible/power-system.md` §24 for days 156 to 220. Chapters 0231 to 0240 are days 231 to 240, which is the seventy-sixth to the eighty-fifth day of the Long Month, and every event in these ten chapters that belongs to a day after 220 has been entered in the fourth form and in no other form, NO FIFTH FORM HAS BEEN ESTABLISHED BY THIS SECTION, and no chapter of this batch carries this line, and a later phase that needs one has to decide it in a place allowed to decide it.**

1. **THE WIND CAME UP OFF ELEVEN MILES OF FLATS AND PUT UP AGAINST THE WALL OF THE STORE ON THE UPPER ROAD, AND IT TOOK THE TOP LINE OFF THE POSTING IN ABOUT FOUR PLACES.** The posting is chalk on a wall at about nine feet with a date at the top of it, that date is the forty-sixth day of the Long Month, and the date is the first thing that goes and the ninth line is under the date and not beside it. The board the man of about twenty-seven washes is about a foot away from the posting and is washed every morning and a board is washed and a page is not washed at all, and **on the eighty-fourth day of the Long Month the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and who wrote the ninth line came up that road with a piece of chalk in his hand and did not put the date back and put the chalk in his pocket.** *Open. A later writer may not put a figure on the wall, may not take the page down, may not move the ninth line, and may not resolve whether the ninth line works. He has still not said whether it works and nobody has asked him in these ten days.*

2. **THE STORE'S OWN BOARD HAS NOT BEEN WRITTEN ON SINCE THE SEVENTIETH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH AND THE FIGURES ON IT ARE THE FIGURES OF THE SIXTY-NINTH DAY OF THAT MONTH.** The figures are therefore seven days old at the seventy-sixth day of that month, eight at the seventy-seventh, fifteen at the eighty-fourth and sixteen at the eighty-fifth, and **every one of those four is on a page and in a section lead or in a mouth, and the last figure anybody has put on that board is for a day that this city has not written down since.** The man of about twenty-seven's reason on the seventieth was that a blank board is a board that says nothing happened on a day; his reason on the seventy-sixth was that a board which has come off a wall in a wind is a board which has not been on a wall, and a page with no date on it and a board with nothing on it are the same thing in two different materials and he would rather have one of them. *Open and a finding about a page and not a document. A later writer may not put the slate back up and may not write a new date on a slate.*

3. **THE SALT WENT HARD AND NOTHING ELSE MOVED, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON THE OPEN HAND BOARD CARRIED THE SAME LUMP AGAINST HIS CHEST FROM THE SIXTY-SECOND DAY OF THE LONG MONTH AND PUT THE BAG DOWN ON THE BOARDS OF THE YARD OF THE STORE ON THE UPPER ROAD ON THE EIGHTY-FIFTH DAY OF THAT MONTH.** He did not open it and he did not break it and nobody asked him to and nobody in that yard picked it up. **The mark the bag left on the front of his coat is still on the coat and the bag is going to leave a mark in those boards in about four days.** *Break is not open. A later writer may not say what a wage in salt is worth, may not have anybody break it in a room, and may not say whether the days still buy a sack.*

4. **A BEAM WITH ONE PAN ON IT HAS STOOD IN THAT YARD SINCE THE SEVENTY-THIRD DAY OF THE LONG MONTH WITH A SACK OF SALT SITTING IN THE ONE PAN, AND THE WIND HAS BEEN AT THAT WALL FOR THE WHOLE OF THE TIME.** The man of about fifty-two who digs made it on the fifty-eighth day of that month, carried it up that road, left it there and has not carried it home. **A sack in an open pan in a yard for twelve days is a sack that is being left out or a sack that is being kept and about four people in this city cannot tell which and about nine of them have not tried.** *Open. A later writer may not replace the pan and may not have him carry the beam back.*

5. **A HOLE NINE FEET FROM THE ONE FOUR PEOPLE MEASURE HAS FILLED IN WITH THE DUST OFF ELEVEN MILES OF FLATS AND IS NOT A HOLE.** The man of about fifty-two who digs put a rod in it on the fifty-ninth day of the Long Month to find out one thing about a sack and has never told anybody what the one thing was, and on the seventy-eighth day of that month there was a skin of it in the bottom of that hole about the depth of a thumb and the rod in it had come up about four inches in its standing. **A woman of about fifty who counts water on the fingers of her left hand will not take a figure off it, because a figure is a thing she takes and there is no figure in a heap of dust, and a hole that four people measure is a column about a hole.** A woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder did not clear it out with her hand and gave the reason out loud, and **the man of about fifty-two who digs has said out loud that he is not going to put a rod in a third hole.** *Open. A later writer may not measure the second hole, may not find out the one thing, and may not say what day the water comes back.*

6. **THE WET STRIPE ON FOUR BOARDS AT THE BOTTOM OF THE REDROOT CUT WENT WITH THE BOARDS AND NOBODY SCRUBBED IT OUT, AND FOUR INCHES OF ONE END OF IT ARE STILL DARKER THAN THE BOARDS TWO INCHES EITHER SIDE.** The stripe was the length and the width of a sack and a corner of the sack sat on one end of it on the sixty-first day of the Long Month, and the boards went the colour of the boards under them on the seventy-fourth day of that month, and the stripe went with them. **A mark somebody put a foot on is a mark that goes slower, and a woman of about thirty-four in that house has said twice in that yard that she is not going to scrub it out, once about a stripe and once about a corner.** *Open. Nobody may count the sacks in that house and nobody may ask, and a chapter may not say whether the days still buy a sack.*

7. **THE KEEPER OF THE REDROOT GATE TURNED HER BOARD FACE DOWN AGAINST HER LEG ON THE EIGHTY-SECOND DAY OF THE LONG MONTH AND DID NOT STOP WORKING THE CHANNEL, AND SHE IS FIFTY-EIGHT DAYS INTO A YEAR OF BEING ANGRY THAT HAS NOT RUN OUT.** The board is not on a list of anything, it carries two dates in two hands and a set of knife marks, **and the twenty-fourth is not on it**, and she has not had it out of her hand since the forty-eighth day of the Long Month and has not put it down once. **It is the second page in this city to go face down since the sixty-fourth day of that month and about four of the four people who saw the first one had been waiting to see whether a second one came, and she said out loud that there is not going to be a second one from her, and she said the last three sentences of it to the boards of the bank and not to anybody.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road saw her do it and said she was not going to say a word about it. *Not a rescue, not an argument, no reason given, no gladness, no child sent, nobody in that yard said her name, and the phrase *the whole of her* is not used about her. A later writer may not attach a weekday to the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month.*

8. **A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-TWO WENT DOWN THE BANK OF A CUT ON THE REACH ON THE EIGHTY-THIRD DAY OF THE LONG MONTH AND HAD NOT COME DOWN IT SINCE BEFORE THE FIFTY-NINTH DAY OF THAT MONTH, AND IT IS THE SEVENTH MONDAY IN A ROW THAT HE HAS STOOD ON THAT BANK.** The six Mondays before that one were the forty-first, the forty-eighth, the fifty-fifth, the sixty-second, the sixty-ninth and the seventy-sixth day of the Long Month, and the nearest of the six was seven days back. He did not come up it, he did not ask a woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder about the refusal she has not shown anybody, and **a man who has carried racks up and down that cut since the second month of this flood did not put the rack in and did not give the reason he has been holding since the seventy-second day of that month.** *Open. A later writer may not have him ask and may not have the rack go in.*

9. **A HAND OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE OPENED ONCE, ON ITS OWN, ON THE BOARDS OF A STALL BY A WHARF ON THE EIGHTY-FIRST DAY OF THE LONG MONTH, AND NOBODY IN THAT MARKET SAID A WORD ABOUT IT.** He has carried that wage four streets twice since the sixty-eighth day of that month and has not opened it, and it came out of the settlement's chest on the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month. **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall left a gap about the width of a person between herself and it and did not touch it, and a girl of seventeen at the rail did not get asked anything and said out loud that a girl of seventeen is not a person anybody comes to with a question.** *Open, and a hand that opens once is not a thing anybody in this city has a word for, and four people in that market are going to write one down.*

10. **THE FIGURES, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM IS DERIVED FROM AN ANCHOR AND NOT FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER.** The Long Month day number is the chapter's own day less 155. The board's figures are for the sixty-ninth day of the Long Month and are seven, eight, fifteen and sixteen days old at Chapters 0231, 0232, 0239 and 0240, and **all four of those values are on a page.** The posting went up on the forty-sixth day of that month and the days it has been up are the day less 201: thirty at Chapter 0231 and thirty-eight at Chapter 0239, and **both of those are on a page** and the rest of the chain is derived. The channel has had no water since the forty-first day of the Long Month, day 196: thirty-five at Chapter 0231 to forty-four at Chapter 0240, and **only the thirty-seventh at 0233, the forty-first at 0237 and the forty-second at 0238 are on a page and the other seven are derived.** The keeper's year began on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, day 179, and her days into it are the Long Month day less twenty-four: fifty-eight at the eighty-second day, and **that is the only value of that chain on a page in these ten chapters.** The share with the post taken out was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and **no figure for it is on a page in these ten chapters and a later writer may not put one there from this section.** The delegation's seventy-two days, the trestle count, the unpaid-days figure and the cost column are on no page in these ten chapters, and the cost column's one paid field is the thirteenth day of the new month and there is no twelfth entry and it was not recited. The wind came up off the flats on the seventy-fourth day of the Long Month and **every mention of it in these ten chapters is written as that day and not as a count of days.** *The count of this city's silences is one hundred and forty-four at day 240, derived, and it is on no page and in no mouth, and the wall said nothing on all ten days.*

11. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE AND DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IN CHAPTERS 0231 TO 0240 WAS RECOMPUTED FROM DAY 1 AND NOT FROM CHAPTER 0230.** Ten chapters, ten days, days 231 to 240: 231 Monday, 232 Tuesday, 233 Wednesday, 234 Thursday, 235 Friday, 236 Saturday, 237 Sunday, 238 Monday, 239 Tuesday, 240 Wednesday. **Every interior weekday word in the ten was extracted by script and read against the day it names and all of them are correct, and no opening paragraph of the ten carries a weekday.** The queue's Tuesdays are the forty-ninth, fifty-sixth, sixty-third, seventieth, seventy-seventh and eighty-fourth days of the Long Month, and **the seventy-seventh is the fifth Tuesday that queue has run on and the eighty-fourth is the sixth, and the seventy-seventh is the third of them with salt in that chest and the eighty-fourth is the fourth.** Day 240 carries one chapter and there is no double day in this batch.

12. **NOTHING IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS IS A POWER.** The gains are a page that has lost its date, a board that has not been written on since the sixtieth day of a month, a hole that has filled in with the wind, four inches of a corner on four boards, a board turned face down against a leg, a hole nobody has measured that is not a hole, a hand that opened once, and a bag put down on boards by a man who is not going to pick it up. **None of them is a power. The compact of eleven lines is not ended. The provisional recognition is not ended. No form for *not recognised* has been found. The seat of holder of the pull is vacant. The delegation of four people and a question has not left, nobody has been asked whether they will go, and no figure in this batch is on it. The room at the far end of the Narrow Mark is unlooked at, in the eleventh batch, and this batch did not go and look. The box is shut. The boards under the chapel are not lifted. The twenty-fifth reading of the join-line is not taken. The trestle book still has a line in it that is not a number and the man keeping the number has still not mentioned it and no chapter of this batch had anybody ask him about it, and the trestle count is on no page in these ten days. The right of refusal that cannot be bought out is not filled and the hole in the Reach's own rule is not filled and the two are not joined, and a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms is not mentioned once in these ten chapters. Marrow is neither convicted nor made useless, is in no chapter of this batch, and nobody asked him about anything. And the System said nothing on all ten days and there is no panel in these ten chapters.**

---

## 31. Rules established in Volume 05, Chapters 0241–0250

**These are additions to the sections above and not replacements. They are on the page in the fifth ten days of Volume 05. §23 is not changed, because §23.19 is the close of Volume 03; §24 is not changed, because §24 is the close of the first twenty chapters of Volume 04; §25 is not changed; §26 is not changed, because §26 is the close of Volume 04; §27 is not changed, because §27 is the close of Volume 05's first ten days, and §27.14's conflict about the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month is still live and is not settled here; §28 is not changed; §29 is not changed; and §30 is not changed. The volume's close record is still `state/volume-04-close.md`, the batch's record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0005.md`, and `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this section does not decide what Volume 05 is.**

**AND THIS BATCH RAN PAST DAY 220 FOR THE THIRD TIME AND FACED THE END OF THE FOURTH DATE FORM AGAIN. The fourth form is *the Nth day of the Long Month* and the Nth is day 155 + N, and it is on the page in §24 for days 156 to 220. Chapters 0241 to 0250 are days 241 to 250, which are the eighty-sixth to the ninety-fifth day of the Long Month, and every event in these ten chapters that belongs to a day after 220 has been entered in the fourth form and in no other form, NO FIFTH FORM HAS BEEN ESTABLISHED BY THIS SECTION, and no chapter of this batch carries this line, and a later phase that needs one has to decide it in a place allowed to decide it.**

1. **THE POSTING ON THE WALL OF THE STORE ON THE UPPER ROAD HAS ONE LINE LEFT ON IT THAT ANYBODY CAN READ FROM THE GROUND, AND IT IS THE NINTH.** The date at the top went in about four places on or about the seventy-sixth day of the Long Month, and the eight lines under it went between the seventy-sixth and the eighty-ninth, and the ninth survived because it was the last one written and it was written with a harder piece of chalk and by a hand that leaned on it. **The wind has been at that wall since the seventy-fourth day of that month and the fine stuff off eleven miles of flats has been going into the face of the chalk the whole time.** *Open. A later writer may not put the date back, may not put a figure on the wall, may not take the page down, may not move the ninth line, may not read the ninth line aloud, and may not resolve whether the ninth line works.*

2. **A LINE IS NOT TESTED BY THE MAN WHO WROTE IT.** The man of about thirty-four who wrote the ninth line gave this out loud in a yard in daylight on the eighty-ninth day of the Long Month, as the reason for his fourth refusal: **it is tested by the people it was written for, and they have to be able to read it themselves and say so in a yard, and if the man who wrote it stands under it and says it works then the only person in four hundred miles who knows whether it works is him.** He refused a fifth time on the ninety-first and said the reason has not changed. *Open, and the reason is his and may not be improved on.*

3. **THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN STOPPED WASHING THAT WALL AND SAID OUT LOUD THAT HE HAD DECIDED NOT TO.** He has washed that board every morning since the seventh week of this flood and has not written on it since the seventieth day of the Long Month, and on the eighty-ninth he stopped throwing water at the wall above it and on the ninety-fifth he said that the stopping was the first decision he had made about that wall since the seventy-sixth. *Open. A later writer may not have him write on the board and may not have him resume the wall.*

4. **A THING IN A WALKING LINE MAKES A LINE, AND TWO LINES THAT MEET MAKE AN ISLAND.** A sack of salt on the boards of that yard since the eighty-fifth day of the Long Month has a track about the width of a boot going round it that was there by the eighth hour of the eighty-sixth, and a queue bent round the same side on the ninety-first, and on the ninety-fifth the two lines met at the south end of it. **The sack is standing on about nine feet of ground that nobody has walked on, and a mark has been in the boards under it since the eighty-ninth day of that month whose length and width are the length and width of the sack.** *Open and not owned. The two people in that yard who could pick the bag up will not, a woman on no list in that building will not put a boot on the nine feet, and nobody may count the boards underneath it, put a day in them, open the bag or break the lump.*

5. **A BREADTH OF GROUND A PERSON KEEPS CLEAR IS A THING THE WEATHER CAN FILL, AND THE ONLY GROUND IN A MARKET A BROOM DOES NOT REACH.** A gap about the width of a person left beside a wage on the boards of a stall by a wharf since the eighty-first day of the Long Month had a finger of the flats in the bottom of it by the eighty-eighth, and the girl of seventeen who sweeps those boards will not move the bag to get at it, because **a broom going over a wage is a thing a person watches**. *Open. A later writer may not have anybody sweep that gap, may not have anybody move that bag, and may not resolve the girl.*

6. **A COIL OF ROPE GOES OVER AN EDGE AND A COIL THAT HAS BEEN IN DUST OPENS WRONG.** A coil on the top length of a rack at the bottom of the Redroot cut went over the edge on the eighty-seventh day of the Long Month and lay three days in the dust of a channel that has had no water in it since the forty-first, and on the ninetieth a man of about thirty-two carried it up and put it on the same top length, tied by a method he said out loud he does not know. **The owner of that rack came to the top of the bank, saw a coil that was not the coil he left, and did not touch it and did not say one word about it, and the rack is still at the edge of the cut and still not in it.** *Open. A later writer may not have the rack go in, may not have the owner ask, and may not have the woman who could carry it up do it.*

7. **AN INSTRUMENT LEFT IN THE OPEN COLLECTS THE DAY, AND WEIGHING WHAT IS UNDER THE DAY IS MEASURING THE YARD INSTEAD OF THE SACK.** The man of about fifty-two who digs put his own coat over the one pan of his beam on the ninety-third day of the Long Month and did not turn the pan out. *Open. A later writer may not replace the pan, may not have the beam carried home, may not have the coat taken off in a chapter, and may not turn the pan out and put a figure on it. **The wind has got under the hem of that coat and lifted it and let it down and the coat has not gone off the pan, because a man held it at two corners until about the eleventh hour and then went up the road, and a later writer may not have the weather holding it down and may not have it go off the pan in a chapter.***

8. **A BROOM PUT DOWN NINE FEET FROM A PERSON WHO IS NOT GOING TO PICK IT UP IS A PAGE WITHOUT A NAME ON IT, AND A SECOND THING IN A PLACE IS NOT AN ANSWER.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road put a broom on the boards at the top of the Redroot cut on the ninety-second day of the Long Month and said nothing about what it was for; the keeper of that gate did not pick it up and did not say thank you and did not say no; and on the ninety-fourth there were two brooms and nobody at the top of that cut would say who brought the second one. *Open. A later writer may not have the keeper pick up a broom, may not have anybody name the person who brought the second, and may not make either broom an answer.*

9. **A THING PUT DOWN IN A PLACE IS READ BY EVERYBODY IN THAT PLACE AFTER FOUR DAYS.** Said out loud in a yard on the eighty-ninth day of the Long Month by the man of about twenty-seven about nine feet of boards, and the finding of the ten days is that **a line in the dirt, a mark in a board, a gap in a floor, a rope on a rack, a broom on a bank and a coat on a pan are all the same kind of object, and that kind of object is a day that nobody chose and cannot be read.** *This is the batch's own finding and it is a finding about a place and not about a document, and it resolves nothing.*

10. **ADRIAN VALE IS IN FIVE OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS AND DID ORDINARY WORK IN FOUR OF THEM, AND HE CAME ELEVEN MILES ON THE NINETY-FOURTH DAY OF THE LONG MONTH TO ASK A QUESTION AND DID NOT ASK IT, AND HE SAID SO OUT LOUD AT THE NINTH HOUR IN DAYLIGHT.** He remains Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing. He carried a full trough into a yard and went round a sack, he came down a bank to a rope that was not his and did not offer to lift it, he stood at a gate with nothing to carry and came anyway, he stood in the only part of a yard a person can stand in and not be in anything, and he stood on a bank for two hours and asked the keeper of the Redroot gate nothing. **He performed no working, no threshold was opened, the other world is not named once in the ten chapters, nobody offered him anything and he asked for nothing, no chapter asked him about a gate, and no chapter turned the name he did not choose into a qualification.** A share with the post taken out was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and **no figure for it is on a page in these ten chapters and the only figure in a mouth for it in this manuscript is fifty-eight, twice, at `chapter-0226.md`, and it is unanswered.**

11. **THE FIGURES, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM IS DERIVED FROM AN ANCHOR AND NOT FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER.** The Long Month day number is the chapter's own day less 155. The board's figures are for the sixty-ninth day of the Long Month and are the Long Month day less sixty-nine: **seventeen days at Chapter 0241, twenty days at Chapter 0244 and twenty-six days at Chapter 0250 are on a page, and the other seven values of that chain are derived.** The board has not been written on since the seventieth day of that month and **no count of days for that fact is on a page in these ten chapters.** The posting went up on the forty-sixth day of that month, day 201, and the days it has been up are the day less 201: forty to forty-nine, and **not one of those is on a page.** The channel has had no water since the forty-first day of the Long Month, day 196, and the days it has been dry are the day less 196: forty-five to fifty-four, and **not one of those is on a page; every mention of the dry channel in the ten chapters is written as the forty-first day of that month.** The keeper's year began on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, day 179, and her days into it are the Long Month day less twenty-four: sixty-two to seventy-one, and **not one of those is on a page, and no chapter of these ten attaches a weekday to the twenty-fourth or counts her days.** The bag went down on the eighty-fifth day of the Long Month and **six days at Chapter 0246 and ten days at Chapter 0250 are on a page.** The rope went over the edge on the eighty-seventh and **three days in the dust at Chapter 0245 are on a page with the day named in the same sentence.** The number in the mouth of the woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road has been there since the forty-seventh day of that month and **forty-five days at Chapter 0247 is on a page.** The hand of about thirty-one opened on the eighty-first day of that month and **IT IS IN NO CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH AND NO COUNT OF DAYS FOR IT IS ON A PAGE IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS: it stood in a first draft of Chapter 0250 in the mouth of the woman of about thirty-four in the store, nine miles from the stall it belongs to, and the review repair pass took it out, and no figure for it may be attributed to this batch or to a mouth.** The queue's Tuesdays are the forty-ninth, fifty-sixth, sixty-third, seventieth, seventy-seventh, eighty-fourth and ninety-first days of the Long Month and **the ninety-first is the seventh at Chapter 0246.** The Mondays on the bank are the forty-first, forty-eighth, fifty-fifth, sixty-second, sixty-ninth, seventy-sixth, eighty-third and ninetieth and **the ninetieth is the eighth, seven times, at Chapter 0245.** **THE COUNT OF THIS CITY'S SILENCES IS ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-TWO AT DAY 250, derived, and it is on no page and in no mouth: it stood at one hundred and forty-four at day 240 and the wall said one thing each on days 242 and 249, and a day the wall says something on is not a silence.** The trestle count, the unpaid-days figure, the delegation's figure and the cost column are on no page in these ten chapters, the cost column's one paid field is the thirteenth day of the new month and there is no twelfth entry and it was not recited.

12. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE AND DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IN CHAPTERS 0241 TO 0250 WAS RECOMPUTED FROM DAY 1 AND NOT FROM CHAPTER 0240.** Ten chapters, ten days, days 241 to 250: 241 Thursday, 242 Friday, 243 Saturday, 244 Sunday, 245 Monday, 246 Tuesday, 247 Wednesday, 248 Thursday, 249 Friday, 250 Saturday. **There are eleven weekday tokens in the ten chapters, seven of them Monday and all seven in Chapter 0245, and every one of the eleven was read against the day it names and every one is correct, and no opening paragraph of the ten carries a weekday.** The map was validated by recomputing days 231 to 240 out of the same two founding figures and reproducing the table in `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0004.md` §3 before it was used on any of this batch's own days. Day 250 carries one chapter and there is no double day in this batch.

13. **THE INSTRUMENT SPOKE TWICE IN TEN CHAPTERS AND IT IS A RULE AND NOT A PROHIBITION.** On day 242, on a bank, when the grit got into the seam of a man's hand: ***A thing that has gone over an edge of its own accord has not been given up by anybody.*** On day 249, on a bank, at the tenth hour, to a man who had a question ready and had had it for an hour: ***You wrote a rule for yourself about asking. A rule you wrote is still a rule when the person in front of you would answer.*** Both are plain, both are one line, both give a rule or a warning, **neither answers anything, and neither is in a chapter whose subject is that a figure cannot be put on a thing.** The wall said nothing on the other eight days of this batch.

14. **NOTHING IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS IS A POWER.** The gains are a page that has lost eight of nine lines, a track in the dirt, an island of ground, a gap with the flats in it, a rope that came back up a bank, a broom nobody picked up, a second broom nobody would account for, a coat over a pan, and a question a man did not ask. **None of them is a power. The compact of eleven lines is not ended. The provisional recognition is not ended. No form for *not recognised* has been found. The seat of holder of the pull is vacant. The delegation of four people and a question has not left, nobody has been asked whether they will go, and no figure in this batch is on it. The room at the far end of the Narrow Mark is unlooked at, in the twelfth batch, and this batch did not go and look. The box is shut. The boards under the chapel are not lifted. The twenty-fifth reading of the join-line is not taken. The trestle book still has a line in it that is not a number and the man keeping the number has still not mentioned it and no chapter of this batch had anybody ask him about it, and the trestle count is on no page in these fifty days. The right of refusal that cannot be bought out is not filled and the hole in the Reach's own rule is not filled and the two are not joined, and a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms is not mentioned once in these ten chapters. Marrow is neither convicted nor made useless, is in no chapter of this batch, and nobody asked him about anything.**

## 32. House formatting rule, established by the review repair pass after the Volume 05 close — NARRATION IS NOT BOLD, AND THE MEASURE IS THE SHARE OF WORDS

**This section was added after `logs/close.review.log` and it is the first rule in this file about how a page is set rather than about what is on it. It is binding on every chapter written after it, and it exists because four volumes broke it and nobody noticed for twenty batches.**

1. **A NARRATIVE PARAGRAPH IS NOT WRAPPED IN BOLD. Bold marks the one speaker in a dialogue, and it is used for a word or two of emphasis and nothing else.** The house pattern is: plain narration, plain speech for the other voice, and the one speaker's speech in bold, with the quotation marks outside the marks — `"**the finding.**"`. A scene lead, a date paragraph, a ledger paragraph, a paragraph of stage direction and a coda are all plain. **Volume 01 is the model: 10.4 per cent of its words sit inside bold, in 847 spans averaging thirty-five words, and eighty-one of its paragraphs carry a whole-paragraph emphasis and they are list markers and one-line beats, not scene leads.** The scene-lead wrap in Volumes 02 to 05 ran at roughly one paragraph in three, which is the density at which emphasis stops being emphasis and becomes the background of the page.

2. **THE MEASURE IS THE SHARE OF A CHAPTER'S WORDS THAT SIT INSIDE BOLD MARKS, AND NOT BOLD MARKS PER SPEECH.** The per-speech measure was used by three state files in Volume 05 against a 52 per cent floor, and it was satisfiable while four-fifths of a page was in bold, **because a bold span in this book is a four-sentence finding and a plain one is a one-line question.** The history and the exact before-and-after figures are at `state/volume-05-close.md` §7.1, and the figures the old measure produced stay on their pages marked superseded, because a record that quietly rewrites itself is the failure this repository names at its own §18.

3. **VOLUME 01 AT 10.4 PER CENT IS THE FIGURE A CHAPTER IS MEASURED AGAIN, AND ABOUT 35 PER CENT IS THE CEILING.** Measured on the files as they stand after the repair pass: Volume 01 **10.4**, Volume 02 **33.0**, Volume 03 **42.3**, Volume 04 **42.7**, Volume 05 **54.0**. **The 2,005 narration paragraphs that carried the wrap are gone and the prose is byte-identical, and a SHA-256 over all 250 chapters with every emphasis marker deleted is the same before and after.** Volume 05's residual is not a markup fault and a writer should not try to repair it by unmarking: **what is left in bold in Volumes 02 to 05 is the one speaker's speech, which is the only speaker marker this manuscript has, and in Volume 05 that speaker runs to eighty-two words a turn against Volume 01's thirty-five.** That is the two-hander exchange and it is a shape fault, and cutting a turn of it is a rewrite.

4. **A SPEECH MAY NOT CARRY TWO SPEAKERS IN ONE EXCHANGE, AND WITHIN A SECTION THE BOLD MARKS ONE VOICE AND THE TWO VOICES ALTERNATE.** This rule is unchanged by anything above and it was installed by an earlier review repair pass. It is a misattribution and not a formatting slip, and it is measured, not asserted.

5. **A SYSTEM PANEL IS PLAIN AND IS NOT BOLD, AND IT IS RARE.** `AGENTS.md` allows at most one per chapter. A panel is set as its own short block with its own line breaks and it carries no emphasis marks, so that a reader can tell at a glance that a thing on the page is not a thing anybody in the world said. **Volume 05 has no panel and no word *System* in it, and a Volume 06 writer who has read `NOVEL_SPEC.md` should know that the genre premise this file's first sections describe is at zero on the page and that the state layer records it as the volume's largest open drift.**

6. **AND A CHAPTER MAY NOT REFER TO ITSELF.** Not *this chapter*, not *this volume*, not *in this batch*, not *the reader*, not *the next four volumes*. Eight such lines existed in seven chapters across Volumes 02 to 05 and all eight are repaired; a manuscript-wide search for the class now returns zero. **Two things are not this rule and are not to be cut.** *This page*, *this book*, *entered in the book*, *on the eleventh page* are the Register, which is a physical book in this world that people enter things in, in rooms, in daylight, with a date on it, and it stands about two hundred times and it is diegetic. And **Volume 02 opens a narrator's frame and says *this volume* thirty-one times across sixteen chapters; that is a decision about a volume's voice rather than a slip, it is left standing on purpose, and whether this book has a narrator who may say it is a live question at `state/volume-05-close.md` §6 and §7.3 for the first phase allowed to decide it.**

---

## 33. DATE-FORM AMENDMENT, decided by the Volume 06 outline phase — THE FOURTH FORM'S RANGE, THE LENGTH OF THE LONG MONTH, AND THE FIFTH FORM

**This section is the calendar and only the calendar. It is a decision of an outline phase, which is the only kind of phase in this repository that may make one, and it is entered here in a numbered subsection so that a batch may not make it and a later writer may check it in one place. Every day figure in it is derived from the two founding figures and from nothing else. Nothing in §§23 to 32 is changed by this section, and in particular §24.1 is left standing with its figure of sixty-five on it, marked superseded, and the page figures of Volume 05 are not disturbed by a word.**

1. **THE FOURTH FORM'S REGISTERED RANGE IS AMENDED IN WRITING FROM DAYS 156–220 TO DAYS 156–255.** The fourth form is *the Nth day of the Long Month* and the Nth is day 155 + N, registered at §24.1 for days 156 to 220 on a sixty-five-day pattern copied from the two months before it. **Batches 0003, 0004 and 0005 of Volume 05 wrote days 221 to 250 in it across thirty pages, and those pages cannot be un-written, and a range that no longer covers the pages written in it is a form that has been lying for thirty-five days.** The amendment is retrospective on the page and dated here and not in a chapter, and **no chapter of Volume 06 may announce the amendment, explain the old range, or date anything in it.**

2. **THE LONG MONTH WAS A HUNDRED DAYS AND NOT SIXTY-FIVE, AND NO PAGE EVER SAID IT WAS SIXTY-FIVE.** Its first day is **day 156**, a Wednesday, by §24.1. Its hundredth and last day is **day 255**, a Thursday. **The sixty-five came from the two months before it and from nothing else: a notary of fifty named the month on a page in a room in daylight with a date on the door on day 156 and registered a span on the pattern of the two months she could see, and the month did not stop at the end of the pattern, and there was no date to stop on and no person to stop it and no form to stop it in.** This is entered here as a fact about the world and not as a fault, and no chapter is asked to say it out loud.

3. **THE FIFTH FORM IS *THE NTH DAY OF THE SHORT MONTH*, THE NTH BEING DAY 255 + N, FROM DAY 256.** The Short Month begins on **day 256, a Friday**, which is the hundred and first day of the Long Month by the fourth form, **and it is a month of sixty days, so it ends on day 315, and day 300 is its forty-fifth day.** The name, the reason, the refusal that precedes it and the reason for that refusal are given on a page in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, by a person, in **Chapter 0256**, and in no other chapter and in no other file. **A name invented by a person in a room is a name an office can use, and in about four months a clerk nine hundred miles away is going to write *the Short Month* at the top of a page and nobody in that building will be able to tell whether it means the month she means** — and that sentence was said in Volume 04 about a different month and it may be said about this one, in this one's own words, once.

4. **THE FIVE FORMS, COMPLETE, AND THE ONLY FIVE.** *The Nth day of this flood* over days 1 to 40. *The Nth day of the new month* over days 26 to 90, the Nth being day 25 + N. *The Nth day of the month after this one* over days 91 to 155, the Nth being day 90 + N. *The Nth day of the Long Month* over days 156 to 255, the Nth being day 155 + N. ***The Nth day of the Short Month*** over days 256 onward, the Nth being day 255 + N. **Never** *of this month*, *of the month*, *of next month*, *of this new month*, or a form like *the last day of the month before this one*. **There are now three month names in this world and not two — *March*, *the Long Month*, *the Short Month* — and that is a change to a fact registered at `outline/volume-04.md` §14, and it is made here and on a page in Chapter 0256 and not by narration. *March* is a month and is never a date number, and the Long Month and the Short Month are capitalised as they are written here.**

5. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE, DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IS DERIVED BY ARITHMETIC AND NEVER FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER OR A DAY MAP.** Day 250 is a Saturday, which is on the page at the end of Volume 05 and which the founding rule gives. **Therefore day 251 is a Sunday.** The seven-day run from day 251 to day 300 is: 251 Sunday, 252 Monday, 253 Tuesday, 254 Wednesday, 255 Thursday, 256 Friday, 257 Saturday, and so on in sevens to 300 Sunday. **The anchors a writer may check against, all derived from day 1 and from nothing else: day 49 a Monday, day 100 a Wednesday, day 130 a Friday, day 131 a Saturday, day 154 a Monday, day 156 a Wednesday, day 179 a Friday, day 200 a Friday, day 250 a Saturday, day 251 a Sunday, day 255 a Thursday, day 256 a Friday, day 275 a Wednesday, day 300 a Sunday.**

6. **THE INTERVAL ANCHORS FOR DAYS 251 TO 300, AND EVERY ONE IS A SUBTRACTION FROM A DAY THE THING HAPPENED, LOOKED UP AND NOT REMEMBERED.** *The Nth day of the Long Month* where the Nth is the chapter's own day less 155, and it is only in range to day 255. *The Nth day of the Short Month* where the Nth is the chapter's own day less 255, and only from day 256. The posting went up on day 201, so its age is the day less 201: **fifty at day 251 and ninety-nine at day 300.** The store's board carries the figures of day 224 and has not been written on since day 225, so the figures are the day less 224 (**twenty-seven at day 251, seventy-six at day 300**) and the board has gone unwritten the day less 225 (**twenty-six at day 251, seventy-five at day 300**). The trestle count is the day less forty: **two hundred and eleven at day 251 and two hundred and sixty at day 300**, and the last two values on a page anywhere in this manuscript are one hundred and sixty-five at `chapter-0205.md:9` and one hundred and seventy-two at `chapter-0212.md:7`, both correct. The first of March is day 85, so the days the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid are the day less eighty-five: **a hundred and sixty-six at day 251 and two hundred and fifteen at day 300.** The channel has had no water since day 196 and the causeway has stood empty since day 197, and **both are written as those days and never as counts.** A share with the post taken out was given on day 168, so its age is the day less 168: **eighty-three at day 251 and a hundred and thirty-two at day 300**, and the only figure in a mouth for it in this manuscript is fifty-eight, twice, at `chapter-0226.md`, and it is unanswered. **The keeper of the Redroot gate's year began on day 179 and the day less 179 is her figure at any day, and it is seventy-six at day 255, seventy-seven at day 256 and a hundred and twenty-one at day 300 — ALL THREE DERIVED, ALL THREE ON NO PAGE AND IN NO MOUTH, AND NO CHAPTER OF VOLUME 06 MAY COUNT HER DAYS, NAME HER YEAR, OR USE HER AT ALL.** The delegation was named on day 145 and its age is the day less 145. The bag went down on day 240, the rope went over the edge on day 242 and came back up on day 245, the brooms were put down on days 247 and 249, the coat went over the pan on day 248, and the wind has been at that wall since day 229. **Every one of these is a subtraction from a day that is on a page in Volume 05 and no chain may be carried from a neighbouring chapter or from a state file.**

7. **AND THE STANDING RULE, WHICH IS THE ONE THIS REPOSITORY KEEPS BREAKING.** *Twenty-one interval faults in Volume 05 Batch 0001, seventeen in 0002, eleven in 0003, nineteen in 0004, seven in 0005 and eleven more in 0005's review repair pass, and this is the largest class of fault in the manuscript and it is one family: a count measured off a day it was not measured from, in a sentence that reads perfectly.* **An outline is where those counts are born. Every figure in item 6 above is the value at a chapter boundary and is derived; a value at a chapter boundary may be used and may not be attributed to a chapter or to a mouth unless a page carries it.**

## 33A. THE MONTH MAP, THE SIXTH FORM, AND THE INTERVAL ANCHORS FOR DAYS 301 TO 350 — decided by the Volume 07 outline phase

**This section was added by the Volume 07 outline phase, which is the second phase in this repository permitted to make a decision, and it is the calendar for the whole of Volume 07. It is a decision of an outline phase and it is entered here in numbered subsections so that a batch may not make it and a later writer may check it in one place. Every day figure in it is derived from the two founding figures and from nothing else. §§1 to 32 are not changed. §33 IS NOT CHANGED, and its five forms, its weekday anchors, its fourth-form amendment and its interval table all stand exactly as the Volume 06 outline phase wrote them. §§34 to 40 are not changed. §§41 to 45 are RESERVED for the five Volume 07 batches, one per ten chapters, and the outline phase wrote no chapter, so they do not exist yet and this section is not one of them.**

1. **THE MONTH MAP, COMPLETE, AND IT IS THE ONLY THING A DAY MAP MAY BE BUILT ON.** Six months of the flood are now accounted for and five of them have a form:

   | The month of the flood | Days | Length | Name on a page | Form and its range |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | the first | **1–25** | — | none | *the Nth day of this flood*, 1–40 |
   | the second | **26–90** | — | *the new month* | *the Nth day of the new month*, N = day − 25 |
   | the third | **91–155** | — | *the month after this one* | *the Nth day of the month after this one*, N = day − 90 |
   | the fourth | **156–255** | a hundred days | **the Long Month** | *the Nth day of the Long Month*, N = day − 155 |
   | the fifth | **256–315** | sixty days | **the Short Month** | *the Nth day of the Short Month*, N = day − 255 |
   | the sixth | **316–** | **of no stated length** | **the Bare Month** | *the Nth day of the Bare Month*, N = day − 315 |

   **TWO FAULTS THIS MAP EXISTS TO STOP, AND BOTH HAVE BEEN PAID FOR IN THIS REPOSITORY.** First, **the fourth month is the Long Month and not the fifth.** `state/volume-06-close.md` §7.1, in the sentence that repaired the Chapter 0300 page fault, calls the Long Month *the fifth month*; by the map in that same file's §4 and by the map above, the second month is the new month, days 26 to 90, the third is days 91 to 155, and the Long Month at days 156 to 255 is the **fourth**. That file's arithmetic about the second month is right and its ordinal for the Long Month is out by one. **The map above is the map both files agree on and the map above is the one.**

   Second, and therefore: **NO CHAPTER OF THIS MANUSCRIPT WRITTEN FROM DAY 301 ONWARD MAY USE AN ORDINAL FOR A MONTH.** Not *the second month*, not *the fourth month*, not *the fifth month*, not *the sixth month*. **THE RULE IS FORWARD-LOOKING AND IT IS NOT A FINDING AGAINST THE THREE HUNDRED CHAPTERS ALREADY WRITTEN, WHICH NOBODY MAY RE-WRITE AND WHICH NOBODY MAY GO BACK AND FIX.** The only two instruments that cannot be got wrong are a count of days of the flood and a named month with its own form, and **the FIVE that carry an ordinal are *this flood*, *the new month*, *the month after this one*, *the Long Month* and *the Short Month* — of which only the last two are named, and the sixth, *the Bare Month*, is a sixth form and is not in that list because a named month of this kind carries no ordinal at all.** *March* is a month and is never a date number. *Never* *of this month*, *of the month*, *of next month*, *of this new month*, or a form like *the last day of the month before this one*.

   **AND THE SEVENTY-SEVEN EXISTING USES, MEASURED, SO THAT A LATER WRITER IS NOT HANDED A RULE THAT LOOKS BROKEN.** A case-insensitive count of the string across the three hundred written chapters gives **sixty-nine *second month*, four *fourth month*, three *third month*, one *fifth month* and no *sixth month*.** **One of them is load-bearing and is the strongest instance in the manuscript: `chapters/volume-04/chapter-0152.md:79` is a PANEL reading *The third month is the Long Month.*** That panel is a page in a finished volume and is not altered here, and the map above is the map it is wrong against, and **that is the whole cost of this decision and it is a page in a done volume and not a batch's problem.** **AND A FALSE POSITIVE NOBODY SHOULD CHASE: `chapters/volume-01/chapter-0042.md:127` reads *a person who says that one night is not enough is a person who wants a second month* — a man wanting a second turn at something, and not an ordinal for a month at all.** A search for these strings has to be read before it is believed, which is `outline/volume-07.md` §17.12 and not a new rule.

2. **THE SIXTH FORM IS *THE NTH DAY OF THE BARE MONTH*, THE NTH BEING DAY 315 + N, FROM DAY 316, AND THE MONTH HAS NO STATED LENGTH.** The Short Month is sixty days and ends on **day 315, a Monday, which is its sixtieth day.** The Bare Month begins on **day 316, a Tuesday.** The name, the reason, the refusal that precedes it and the reason for the refusal are given on a page in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, by a person, in **Chapter 0316**, and in no other chapter and in no other file, and the person is **Sera Vail, a notary of fifty, twenty-six years a notary**, who named the Short Month on day 256 on the same reasoning and who says the cost out loud first and who is refused by a person before she does it.

   **AND NO LENGTH IS STATED, AND THAT IS THE DECISION INSIDE THE DECISION.** §33.2 records that the Long Month was a hundred days and that nobody ever said it was sixty-five, because a notary registered a span on the pattern of the two months she could see and the month did not stop at the end of the pattern. **The Bare Month is registered with no span at all: the notary names it and declines to say how long it is, and says why, and the reason is hers.** Consequences, and they bind: a chapter may write an ordinal for any day of it; **no chapter may say what the month does at its end, how many days it has, or whether it is long or short**; and a later volume that needs a sixth month form may not assume this one has sixty days in it or a hundred. **A named month of no stated length is the cheapest protection in this section and it costs one sentence in a notary's mouth.**

3. **THERE ARE NOW FOUR MONTH NAMES IN THIS WORLD AND NOT TWO, and that is a change to a fact registered at `outline/volume-04.md` §14, made here and on a page in Chapter 0316 and not by narration: *March*, *the Long Month*, *the Short Month*, *the Bare Month*.**

4. **A WEEKDAY IS A FIGURE, DAY 1 IS A TUESDAY, AND EVERY WEEKDAY IN DAYS 301 TO 350 IS DERIVED BY ARITHMETIC AND NEVER FROM A NEIGHBOURING CHAPTER, A CARD, A DAY MAP OR A STATE FILE.** **Day 300 is a Sunday, which is on the founding rule and on the page of Chapter 0300, and therefore day 301 is a Monday.** Day 315 is a Monday and day 316 is a Tuesday. Day 350 is a Monday. **The run, all fifty, and every one of them was recomputed from day 1 and from nothing else and the run was validated by recomputing days 251 to 300 out of the same two founding figures and reproducing §33.5's published run before it was used on any day of this volume's own:**

5. **THE INTERVAL ANCHORS FOR DAYS 301 TO 350, extending item 6 above in the same order and in the same numbered form, and every one of them is a subtraction from a day the thing happened, looked up in a file and not remembered.** A day of this flood is inclusive. The Long Month day and the Short Month day are as in item 4 above. **Every value in this item is derived, and a derived value may be used in a chapter that dates it and may not be attributed to a chapter or to a mouth unless a page carries it.** The two values are at day 301, the volume's first day, and at day 350, its last, and the third is at the day this outline names.
